    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nUnless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}\n\\label{sec:count_curves}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{\\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves for NER and RE. Error bars show $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds (42, 123, 456) $\\times$ 25 batches per seed. RE accuracy declines monotonically with count; NER recall exhibits a U-shaped pattern with minimum at intermediate counts. Full-schema points (rightmost, hollow markers) represent the complete type inventory with no batch sampling variance. \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty. The pattern replicates across three seeds (42, 123, 456; max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD); intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$ at every count level (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$, pooled across three seeds). Count curves (25 batches per level) establish trend direction with error bars; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$)---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \\ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) is excluded as RE's per-instance format precludes direct comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]; both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0]), roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Conditional pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ unconditional $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}; we note the conditioning inflates this estimate) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, consistent with a Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; see Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim} for the illustrative aggregation model).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r < -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded interaction in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

73	Under the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. Error bars show $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds (42, 123, 456) $\\times$ 25 batches per seed. RE accuracy declines monotonically with count; NER recall exhibits a U-shaped pattern with minimum at intermediate counts. Full-schema points (rightmost, hollow markers) represent the complete type inventory with no batch sampling variance. \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows RE accuracy declines monotonically with schema size (0.86$\\to$0.50 for FewRel). NER recall declines through intermediate counts (0.51$\\to$0.27, plateauing across counts 22--44) before partial recovery at full-schema (0.41), where the elimination of type-filtering overhead restores extraction performance. DocRED replicates the monotonic RE pattern (88.0\\%$\\to$38.7\\%, $-$56\\%; Appendix). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty. The pattern replicates across three seeds (42, 123, 456; max $\\Delta = 10.7$\\,pp, within 1\\,SD); intra-batch variance exceeds inter-seed variance by ${>}$5$\\times$ at every count level (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}). Formal trend analysis confirms monotonic RE decline (Jonckheere-Terpstra $J = 109{,}707$, $p < 0.001$, pooled across three seeds). Count curves (25 batches per level) establish trend direction with error bars; magnitude estimation relies on the higher-powered BCS-controlled experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate (the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$)---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \\ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) is excluded as RE's per-instance format precludes direct comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]; both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0]), roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Conditional pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ unconditional $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}; we note the conditioning inflates this estimate) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, consistent with a Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; see Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim} for the illustrative aggregation model).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Dimension~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Dimension~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r < -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded interaction in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0]), roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Conditional pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ unconditional $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}; we note the conditioning inflates this estimate) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, consistent with a Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; see Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim} for the illustrative aggregation model).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Dimension~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Dimension~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$), modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}). The BCS--accuracy correlation holds across four structurally distinct prompt variants at count 32 (all $r < -0.47$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}), confirming that the confusability effect is not an artifact of prompt design.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded interaction in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

Error bars show $\\pm 1$\\,SD across 3 seeds (42, 123, 456) $\\times$ 25 batches per seed. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \\ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) is excluded as RE's per-instance format precludes direct comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]; both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). Notably, the independent MiniLM embedding yields a \\emph{stronger} SCS--confusion correlation on Few-NERD ($r = 0.172$ vs.\\ Qwen3 $r = 0.138$; Appendix), suggesting that shared-representation bias is conservative rather than inflationary.\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Conditional pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ unconditional $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}; we note the conditioning inflates this estimate) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, consistent with a Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; see Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim} for the illustrative aggregation model).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Dimension~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Dimension~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$), modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two dimensions: \\textbf{Dimension~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Dimension~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), consistent with bounded association in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. The full-range regression ($n = 69$, five count levels) serves as the primary separability test; the $2 \\times 2$ factorial ($n = 54$) as a sensitivity check. TOST equivalence testing confirms bounded interaction (interaction = 0.47\\,pp, $p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}\nTraining-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \\cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \\cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \\cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \\cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \\cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}\nThe most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms. Concurrently, \\citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \\emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}---both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}\nOur centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \\cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.\n\n",

For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.",
          " ",

Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms. Concurrently, \\citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \\emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}\nTraining-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \\cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \\cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \\cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \\cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \\cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}\nThe most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification (not partitioning quality) is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Specifically, LLM-PEE employs union merge across sub-schema extractions, which our \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd} analysis shows causes precision to collapse from 0.56 to 0.21 in NER as false positives accumulate across passes; RE avoids this because closed-set classification does not require span reconciliation, explaining why LLM-PEE's decomposition succeeds for relation/event tasks but faces unresolved merge challenges on span-level extraction. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms. Concurrently, \\citet{haystack-to-needle-2025} reduces label spaces via iterative LLM filtering, an engineering complement to our analytical approach. Our framework explains \\emph{why} such reduction works: it implicitly lowers batch confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion. The relationship between label similarity and classification confusion has precedent in traditional ML: hierarchical classification suffers sibling confusion proportional to inter-class proximity \\citep{deng-2010-imagenet}, label confusion matrices improve text classification \\citep{guo-2021-label-confusion}, and classification confusion captures semantic similarity \\citep{zhou-2025-rethinking}. Hierarchical label sets reduce class confusion in zero-shot image classification \\citep{novack-2023-chils}; enriched type descriptions improve zero-shot NER \\citep{cocchieri-2025-zeroner}---both orthogonal to our confusability-aware batching.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work. The modest pairwise effect size ($r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$) is consistent with standards for individual-difference predictors in behavioral research \\citep{cohen-1988-power}, where such magnitudes are considered ``small but real'' effects that compound at the aggregate level \\citep{lakens-2013-effect-size}.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}\nOur centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \\cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.\n\n",

  "uuid": "daaadcf5-4e97-4c1c-8780-d798724843de",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/sdgd_negative.tex",
    "oldString": "confusability-driven degradation",
    "newString": "confusability-associated degradation",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n\\label{sec:sdgd}\n\nA natural intervention against confusability-driven degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Merge amplification in NER.}\nThe core failure mechanism is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities.\n\nQuantitatively, single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge across 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\\to$ 0.314 for sdgd\\_zero, 0.446 for random batch). The net F1 loss is substantial: 0.445 $\\to$ 0.251 (sdgd\\_zero) or 0.320 (random batch). Per-type extraction (66 batches) pushes recall to 0.586 but precision to 0.194 (F1\\,=\\,0.292), confirming that the precision--recall imbalance intensifies with the number of passes. Random batching outperforms confusability-aware SDGD on NER (0.320 vs.\\ 0.251) because the dominant degradation source---merge amplification---is independent of batch composition; any multi-pass scheme suffers from it regardless of grouping quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence gating does not resolve merge amplification.}\nConfidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}\nMerge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this mechanism entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n\n\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\nOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n\nHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

Random batching outperforms confusability-aware SDGD on NER (0.320 vs.\\ 0.251) because the dominant degradation source---merge amplification---is independent of batch composition; any multi-pass scheme suffers from it regardless of grouping quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence gating does not resolve merge amplification.}\nConfidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}\nMerge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this mechanism entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n\n\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\nOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n\nHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n\\label{sec:sdgd}\n\nA natural intervention against confusability-associated degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent.     "newString": "a key predictor of degradation in RE",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}\n\\label{sec:sdgd}\n\nA natural intervention against confusability-associated degradation is to partition the schema so that confusable types never co-occur in the same prompt. We test this idea with \\textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD\\footnote{SDGD (Schema Decomposition and Grouping for Degradation mitigation) is an adopted abbreviation for schema decomposition and grouping; the graph structure is undirected.}), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches.\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a batch. Each color class forms one extraction batch. For NER, predictions from all batches are merged via set union; for RE, each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type (oracle routing). We test four NER modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, each type extracted in isolation. Every mode falls below the single-pass NER baseline: SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251, random batching 0.320, per-type 0.292, versus 0.445 for single-pass (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Merge amplification in NER.}\nThe core failure mode is \\emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text to the model $k$ times with different type subsets causes each pass to independently generate entity predictions, and union merge accumulates all of them into a single output. Each pass produces an independent set of span predictions; because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes \\cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}, the same span may receive conflicting type labels from different passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge retains all predictions, so false positives scale with $k$ while true positives are bounded by the fixed set of gold entities.\n\nQuantitatively, single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge across 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\\to$ 0.314 for sdgd\\_zero, 0.446 for random batch). The net F1 loss is substantial: 0.445 $\\to$ 0.251 (sdgd\\_zero) or 0.320 (random batch). Per-type extraction (66 batches) pushes recall to 0.586 but precision to 0.194 (F1\\,=\\,0.292), confirming that the precision--recall imbalance intensifies with the number of passes. Random batching outperforms confusability-aware SDGD on NER (0.320 vs.\\ 0.251) because the dominant degradation source (merge amplification) is independent of batch composition; any multi-pass scheme suffers from it regardless of grouping quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence gating does not resolve merge amplification.}\nConfidence-based gating (retaining only predictions above threshold) trades recall for precision but does not recover net F1 (0.251$\\to$0.241), confirming that merge artifacts are systemic rather than confined to low-confidence predictions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}\nMerge amplification is specific to span-level tasks. Each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. RE avoids this failure mode entirely: under oracle routing, each relation instance receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, requiring no span reconciliation and eliminating the multi-pass interference that causes SDGD to fail on NER. This makes RE a clean testbed for evaluating whether confusability-aware decomposition improves accuracy once merge artifacts are removed.\n\n\\enlargethispage*{9\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Oracle routing as performance ceiling.}\nOracle routing routes each test instance to the batch containing its gold type, eliminating cross-type confusion within each batch. On FewRel 80-relation RE, oracle-routed decomposition achieves 0.802 accuracy versus 0.504 for the single-pass baseline---a gain of +29.8\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). This is the strongest positive finding of the decomposition experiments: it demonstrates that type confusability is indeed a key driver of degradation in RE, and that performance recovers substantially when confusable types are separated.\n\nHowever, oracle routing requires gold labels and is not deployable. The gap between the oracle ceiling and practical routing reveals an important asymmetry: while \\emph{any} decomposition benefits enormously from removing the 80-type bottleneck (+28.4\\,pp for random batching, +29.8\\,pp for graph coloring), the \\emph{additional} benefit of confusability-aware grouping over random grouping is modest (+1.4\\,pp, $p \\approx 0.19$). The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds, limited by the narrow BCS margin (0.006) between graph-coloring and random batches at this partition granularity. Finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to translate the oracle-demonstrated potential into significant practical gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical routing strategies.}\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using a coarser 5-group decomposition (oracle ceiling: 72.4\\%, +22.0\\,pp; cf.\\ +29.8\\,pp under 17-group decomposition in Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}): max-confidence (all predictions), max-confidence (positive only), none-aware selection, margin-based routing, and random assignment. All five perform below the single-pass baseline (50.4\\%), with the best practical strategy reaching only 36.5\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_routing}). The root cause is constrained decoding: the enum constraint forces the model to produce a confident type prediction for every sub-schema, even when the correct type is absent from the candidate set. As a result, 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, and the none-rate per sub-schema is only 20--40\\%. Because every sub-schema produces high-confidence outputs, confidence signals become unreliable and all confidence-based routing fails. Free-form generation or calibrated confidence estimation may provide more reliable routing signals for future work.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds. Graph coloring yields +1.4\\,pp over random batching ($p \\approx 0.19$, n.s.) with a narrow BCS margin (0.006). The +29.8\\,pp gap between single-pass and decomposed conditions confirms that type separation recovers performance when merge amplification is absent.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n",

If No, a justification is provided.\n\n\\subsection*{A \\quad For Every Submission}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=\\textbf{A\\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,itemsep=4pt]\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you describe the limitations of your work?}\n\nYes. The Limitations section (after \\S\\ref{sec:conclusion}) discusses single-model reliance, modest pairwise effect sizes, cross-pool slope consistency, factorial interaction tests with TOST equivalence, decomposition scope, and metric scope.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you discuss any potential risks of your work?}\n\nN/A. This is a purely analytical study of schema-induced degradation in existing LLM IE systems. It does not introduce new models, datasets, or deployment-facing artifacts. The findings characterize failure modes and are intended to inform safer schema design.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Do the abstract and introduction summarize the paper's main claims?}\n\nYes. The abstract and \\S\\ref{sec:introduction} state the two-channel degradation pattern (count and confusability), the cross-model consistency check, and the negative SDGD result.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Did you use AI assistants (e.g., ChatGPT, Copilot) when working on this paper?}\n\nYes. All four datasets are publicly available research benchmarks. Few-NERD is released under CC-BY-SA 4.0; FewRel, DocRED, and MAVEN are publicly released for research use.
